##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v1 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology.

##### **Edge-Gradient Augmentation under Reinforcement Learning for Temporal Graph**
2611.16678v1 by Yongqing Zhou, Jiawei Chen, Wenjun Wang, Xinyu Wang, Wei Yu, Qiyao Peng

Temporal link prediction is critical for understand- ing dynamic interactions in evolving networks, informing appli- cations ranging from social network analysis to recommendation systems. Existing methods typically rely on raw temporal graph data, often neglecting explicit edge-change information that can significantly improve predictive performance. To address this gap, we propose edge-gradient reinforced learning (EG-RL), a framework that explicitly encodes edge-gradient events (edge births and deaths) through dedicated node and link encoding modules. EG-RL uses a policy-driven reinforcement-learning mechanism to dynamically select and augment the most infor- mative edge snapshots, guided by critic-based feedback to opti- mize prediction accuracy. The model comprises distinct modules for node encoding, link encoding, token mixing, and channel mixing, integrated through global pooling and followed by a two-layer multilayer perceptron classifier. Extensive experiments on multiple dynamic graph datasets demonstrate substantial gains in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve and average precision. Ablation studies further confirm the necessity of adaptive edge-gradient augmentation and validate the effectiveness of the proposed encoding and reinforcement- learning mechanisms.

##### **Studies on Sign Language Retrieval in Low-Resource Dictionary Settings**
2611.16621v1 by Ushnish Sarkar, Vineet Kumar Rakesh, Bhaswar Chattopadhyay, Tapas Samanta

Most sign languages have no annotated corpus, only small dictionaries with one or a few example videos per sign. This study examines how well a pose encoder trained on a single sign language serves such languages without any target-language training.
  An encoder trained with metric learning on American Sign Language (ASL-Citizen) is evaluated frozen on six datasets of Turkish, Russian, Indian, Argentinian, Pakistani and Sri Lankan sign languages, with one or four clips per sign enrolled and, where
  signer identities are available, the query's signer excluded from the gallery. With one enrolled clip, the encoder retrieves the correct sign first in 36.4-80.7 % of queries, against 16.0-69.6 % for the best learning-free method (angle-based dynamic

  choices matter: a per-pair InfoNCE objective outperforms supervised contrastive and cross-entropy training, and blocking attention between body, left hand and right hand inside the encoder raises accuracy by up to 5.1 points. The best configuration
  reaches 68.0 % one-shot accuracy (59.0 % for the reference encoder). On CISLR, an Indian Sign Language dictionary of 4,765 words with one video per word, the encoders find the correct word first for 17.3-19.0 % of queries, above the 16.8 % of the
  original RGB baseline. Over eight seeds with the joint encoder, spherical, Euclidean and hyperbolic embedding spaces differ by at most 1.6 points, except the hyperbolic token-attention head trained with InfoNCE, which is 1.4-2.0 points worse, so the

##### **A Dual-Branch Graph and Convolutional Network for Interpretable Movement EEG Classification**
2611.16609v1 by Maryam Ostadsharif Memar, Behzad Nazari

Decoding upper-limb movements from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) is an important problem for brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), but many deep learning models provide limited insight into the brain regions underlying their predictions. We propose a two-branch architecture that combines an LGGNet-inspired local-global graph branch with an EEGNet-style convolutional branch for movement-versus-rest classification. The graph branch groups 61 EEG electrodes into anatomically informed regions and learns their spatial relationships, providing a direct representation of the regions emphasized by the model. We evaluate the proposed architecture using leave-one-subject-out cross-validation on the Ofner upper-limb movement dataset. The model achieves a mean F1-score of 0.74 +/- 0.06 across subjects. Beyond classification performance, we analyze the learned graph structure to identify the regions with the strongest connectivity. Across all held-out subjects, the highest connectivity is consistently observed in the Parietal Left Low region, corresponding to electrodes P1 and P3. This region is opposite to the performed right-arm movements and lies within the posterior parietal area previously associated with upper-limb movement processing. These results show that incorporating a region-based graph into the decoder can provide an anatomically interpretable representation while maintaining effective movement classification, offering a practical approach toward more transparent EEG-based BCIs.

##### **AI has its own style in mathematical proving**
2611.16527v1 by Xiaokai Li, Ziyu Zhou, Qibin Cheng, Yangcheng Wu, An Zeng, Ying Fan

As large language models increasingly generate mathematical proofs, whether they develop a proving style distinct from that of human mathematicians remains an open question. This question is particularly pressing as mathematicians increasingly voice concerns that proofs generated by large language models are difficult to interpret. Here we ask three large language models to independently reprove all 465 propositions of Euclid's \textit{Elements} and compare their citation choices against the human-proof baseline through Jaccard similarity and complex network analysis. We find that AI-generated proofs differ from human proofs, and this difference is stable and reproducible: mean pairwise Jaccard similarity among AI runs (~0.68) is nearly twice that between AI and human proofs (~0.37), and cross-run classification consistency is also high. By examining citation structure in detail, we identify the concrete signature of this difference: approximately 40% of AI citations point to foundational elements (definitions, common notions, and postulates), compared with only 13\% in human proofs; even among theorem-to-theorem citations, AI tends to cite more distant results. Roughly half of AI's extra citations trace to the upstream dependencies of the human proof. Together, these findings suggest that the AI proving style is a tendency to construct arguments on its own from foundational elements rather than directly citing later, more established results, which we term "building from the ground up". Our findings offer a new perspective on how AI systems organise and invoke mathematical knowledge, with implications for understanding the interpretability of AI-generated proofs.

##### **Lost in Delegation: Studying the Effectiveness of Task Orchestration in Generative Search**
2611.16526v1 by Tamara Stojanovska, Elisabeth Kirsten, Leon Swazinna, Muhammad Bilal Zafar

LLMs have quickly evolved from models used in isolation to agentic systems that delegate tasks to external tools and other LLMs to solve complex assignments. This agentic augmentation has led to impressive capability gains. However, when and how delegation improves performance remains poorly understood. For example, it is unclear whether delegation helps when tasks involve post-cutoff information that the orchestrating LLM cannot reliably resolve from its own knowledge. In this work, we use purpose-built datasets with pre- and post-cutoff splits to compare various delegation modes. We find that delegation provides no consistent accuracy improvement over non-delegated setups, with effects varying across models and tasks. When tasks involve knowledge unavailable to the orchestrator, delegation can substantially degrade accuracy, while direct tool use with the same LLM performs much better. Our analysis identifies distorted delegated queries, inefficient tool use, and failures to preserve and use information between agents as recurring failure modes. These findings suggest that effective orchestration requires both well-calibrated delegation decisions and effective exchange of information between agents.

##### **A Joint LLM and Optimization Agent Solution for Drone Scheduling in Victim Search Missions**
2611.16524v1 by Yinghao Zhang, Yalin Liu, Hong-Ning Dai, Yan Zhang, Qubeijian Wang, Bishenghui Tao, Yaohan Li

Victim search in wilderness areas is time-critical. Drones enable rapid area search, but scheduling them is challenging because victims may continue moving, detection results are uncertain, and effective decisions must combine structured mission information with unstructured contextual clues and domain knowledge. To address this, we propose an agent-aided drone scheduling system. The system employs an agent at the ground control station (GCS) to coordinate multiple drones over successive search cycles. In each cycle, the agent applies a scheduling policy that combines a large language model (LLM) for reasoning about where to search with an optimizer for determining how to dispatch the drones under energy constraints. Simulation results show that the proposed system consistently completes the victim-search mission, whereas none of the six non-LLM baselines does so and direct LLM scheduling is considerably less reliable. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/1643204431/llm-uav-victim-search.

##### **ReA-Bench: Benchmarking Responsible Agent Auditing**
2611.16493v1 by Zipeng Ye, Shenyu Dai, Yong Xie, Kun Wang, Jiyang Guan, Zhikai Chen, Jiaqi Weng, Jialing Tao, Hui Xue

As agents perform tasks autonomously in local and cloud environments, auditing their execution trajectories can help service providers identify risks and improve agent harnesses and foundation models. Our analysis of production and red-team trajectories shows that some risks are repeatedly missed across auditing configurations. Overall recall obscures which risks are missed and whether higher recall comes with more false positives on legitimate behavior. Existing benchmarks offer limited insight into these failures. Generated trajectories can contain inconsistent tool calls and responses, conspicuous risky actions, or repetitive attack patterns. Grouping results by risk type also makes it difficult to tell whether auditors overlooked evidence, accepted an unsupported conclusion, or lacked information needed to make a judgment.
  We introduce ReA-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for auditing responsible agent behavior. It organizes auditing challenges into six tiers: internal safety knowledge, contradictions within trajectories, evidence retrieval in long contexts, verification of sources and reasoning, indirect steering, and missing information. It includes challenging benign cases in which actions resemble risky ones but are consistent with the task and its authorization. It also provides paired trajectory versions to test whether auditors detect the same risks more reliably when the trajectory is shortened or missing information is supplied. Most samples are drawn from real production trajectories, with scenarios manually selected and adapted to preserve realistic tasks and risks that could plausibly arise during execution.

##### **HeMeR: Heterogeneous Memory Reconciliation for Embodied Agents via Structured KV Reuse**
2611.16487v1 by Saehun Chun, Wonje Choi, Jinwoo Jang, Taeyoon Kwack, Honguk Woo

Embodied agents make decisions by combining information from heterogeneous memory modules, including declarative memory for explicit knowledge, procedural memory for learned routines, and working memory for current observations. During interaction, the memory contents required for decision making change with the agent's state, goal, and task progress, while many contents remain shared across consecutive decisions. However, existing memory modules are typically accessed through separate interfaces, so each updated memory context is often processed as a new input to the foundation model. This prevents selective key-value (KV) cache reuse for unchanged memory contents and forces full KV cache recomputation even when much of the memory context remains valid. We introduce HeMeR, an inference framework that performs KV reconciliation between dynamically updated memory contexts and reusable KV states. HeMeR tracks declarative, procedural, and working memory contents across action decisions, preserves KV states for unchanged contents, and reconciles only the KV states affected by memory-content updates. This enables efficient and stable inference as memory contents evolve during interaction. We evaluate HeMeR on ALFWorld, Habitat, and real-world robotic deployments requiring coordinated use of heterogeneous memory contents. In the real-world setting, HeMeR achieves 83.20% task success and reduces per-step inference time from 2.94 to 1.87 seconds compared with MemRL, while preserving compatible KV states as task progress updates the memory context.

##### **PhysCapBench: A Cross-Domain Benchmark for Physically Grounded Video Captioning**
2611.16424v1 by Haofeng Wang, Pengcheng Wen, Ruiqi Li, Yuhao Zhang, Tiange Zhang, Boren Zheng, Xuanyi Liu, Siyu Tao, Danni Yu, Gan Lin, Haocheng Tang, Tianzizhe Guo, Mingyuan Su, Boyuan Chen, Zhongyuan Wang

Physical AI aims to learn transferable physical knowledge from real-world experience to understand, predict, and act in the physical world. Internet videos and embodied demonstrations provide complementary observations of motion, interaction, and state evolution. A foundational data challenge is to measure and preserve this physical information as training supervision. In video-language pretraining, captions express observed processes in language, yet a semantically correct description can omit the interactions and transitions that define those processes. Rich physical experience in video does not automatically translate into sufficient language supervision. This gap calls for a yardstick to assess annotation quality, compare captioning strategies, and guide training-data refinement. Motivated by our data construction for world-model pretraining, we introduce PhysCapBench, to our knowledge the first unified physical-caption benchmark spanning all three domains: third-person scenes, egocentric manipulation, and handheld-gripper demonstrations. It provides human-verified annotations for 1,500 video sequences and evaluates accuracy and coverage through seven physical dimensions. ##### **HyBridge: Bridge-Guided Self-Supervised Hyperbolic Hypergraph Representation Learning**
2611.16351v2 by So-Bin Jung, David Yoon Suk, Sang-Wook Kim

Hypergraphs capture groupwise relationships among multiple nodes and often exhibit tree-like structures. Although hyperbolic space is well suited for modeling such structures, simply extending existing hypergraph representation learning (HRL) methods to hyperbolic space does not consistently improve performance. In this paper, we investigate the relationship between node structural properties and hyperbolic embedding radii and find that bridge nodes tend to lie closer to the center in graphs, whereas this relationship is not consistently observed in hypergraphs. We further find that conventional bridge score estimation may fail to capture the groupwise connectivity of hypergraphs. Motivated by these findings, we propose HyBridge, a self-supervised hyperbolic HRL framework that (1) estimates node bridge scores by considering the external connectivity of individual hyperedges, (2) learns node and hyperedge embeddings through hyperbolic hypergraph encoding, (3) guides node radial positions according to their bridge scores, and (4) employs contrastive learning to preserve node discriminability. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world hypergraphs demonstrate that HyBridge outperforms 22 competing methods, achieving performance improvements of up to 9.82% in node classification. For reproducibility, we provide the code at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/HyBridge/.

##### **Does Deep Knowledge Tracing Learn Semantic Relations Between Knowledge Components?**
2611.16323v1 by Kia Karbasi, Mahdi Saiedi, Kevin Hong, Gregory Pottie

Deep Knowledge Tracing (DKT) is a knowledge tracing (KT) model that updates a shared latent state from sequences of interactions, each pairing a knowledge component (KC) with a response. An interaction with one KC can therefore affect predictions for other KCs. We ask: Are the KC relations learned by DKT semantic? We construct text-derived semantic KC representations using nine pipelines to test whether the conclusions hold across alternative ways of constructing those representations.
  The first test compares DKT-derived KC relation graphs with semantic relation structures induced by pairwise cosine similarities among the KC representations. Across the evaluated datasets, embedding models, and pipelines, the two structures align only weakly and inconsistently. The second test introduces semantic information into DKT; the pretrained text-derived representations do not consistently outperform the standard DKT baseline.

KC-supervised fine-tuned BERT produces small positive AUC differences in some matched comparisons, but the study does not establish their stability across runs. These effects remain limited and inconsistent. The findings suggest that behavioral, temporal, structural, or psychometric signals may also shape DKT's learned relations. DKT-derived relations are not primarily semantic: the tested text-derived semantic structures do not strongly recover the DKT-derived relation structure.

##### **ORCHID-SAGE: Fully Reproducible CALPHAD Assessment from Evidence to Phase Diagram**
2611.16308v1 by Raymundo Arróyave

Knowledge of phase stability is the first requirement for materials design, and predicting the conditions under which new materials can be synthesized has become a key capability of self-driving laboratories (SDLs). For inorganic materials, notably alloys, these predictions come from thermodynamic databases built with the CALPHAD method. Building a database, however, requires expert judgment at many steps, and because these decisions are rarely recorded, others can use a database but cannot regenerate or update it. Machines can already collect the data, build a first model with machine-learned interatomic potentials and refine it against experiments. The assessment itself, the sequence of judgments that turns these parts into a reliable database, is still carried out by human experts. We argue that it can be automated reproducibly only if the expert knowledge behind an assessment is encoded in formal protocols, so that an assessment delivers a record of its construction and not only its parameters. We introduce ORCHID-SAGE, a standard that organizes this record into reproducible and automatable protocols. In this first attempt, applied to Cr-Ni, the database was regenerated from its record by a script on a second machine, by an AI agent working only through the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and by a refit from public sources only, with the same predictions. Recorded in this way, a database need no longer be fixed at publication but can be updated by AI agents as SDLs and other sources provide new evidence, while the judgment remains with people and on the record.

##### **Causals: An Open Knowledge Graph for Research on Causal Reasoning**
2611.16267v1 by Miaozhe Lin, Qiongzheng Lin, Zhongwei Liang, Zijun Liu

Applying causal knowledge to a new event requires connecting the event to relevant entities and the relationships through which its consequences may unfold. We present Causals, an open knowledge graph for research on this form of reasoning. Its initial release, constructed from news reports, contains 369,184 nodes and 1,272,418 edges linking reported events, organizations, commodities, financial instruments, and other entities. Structural relationships, observations, and attributed causal claims retain distinct semantics and links to source records. We describe the construction and representation of the resource and characterize its connectivity and multi-hop retrieval properties. Causals provides shared relational knowledge for studying how language models retrieve relevant context and apply mechanisms to particular events. The dataset is available under CC BY 4.0 at https://causals.org/, and we invite contributions to its ontology, records, and evidence.

##### **Runtime Analysis of Cartesian Genetic Programming on Max: A Proven Exponential Speedup**

Genetic Programming (GP) is a search paradigm inspired from natural evolution for the automated discovery of expressions, functions, and computer programs. Cartesian GP (CGP) is a flavor of GP that uses a graph-based model to encode candidate programs, as contrasted to the conventional Tree-based GP (TGP). Since its inception two decades ago, CGP has predominantly been analyzed empirically, thus little is known regarding its theoretical performance guarantees. This paper analyzes CGP for the Max problem, which has been only rigorously studied for TGP with a proven expected runtime superlinear in the optimal program length $n$. We prove that the expected time for the ($1$+$1$)~CGP employing common mutation operators to evolve an equivalent optimal of the same output is polylogarithmic in $n$, specifically at most $O(\log^9{n})$. This is remarkable, as it shows an exponential speedup by switching to CGP. Our results shed light on the benefit and the compactness of the graph-based representation for programs, and on how CGP can navigate its search and fitness spaces efficiently. ##### **When Local Definitions Are Missing: Stable Errors in Language Models**
2611.15597v1 by Zengqing Wu, Chuan Xiao

Contracts, technical manuals, and online communities often give familiar words special meanings and define them in one place. We study this with multiple-choice questions whose correct answers depend on a local definition. The questions come from a synthetic vocabulary, commercial contracts, Reddit community glossaries, and the documentation of a decentralized exchange. We ask three language models each question with and without the definition. Without using the answer key, we then test whether their repeated answers and stated confidence reveal their own errors. Separately, we give a small open model the answer key and test whether it can predict which questions larger models get wrong. We report three findings. (1) Familiar words that a source redefines cause the most errors. Models often repeat the same wrong answer, and neither repeated answers nor stated confidence reliably flag it. (2) The source definition fixes most errors on synthetic questions, but a definition drawn from the model's own prior knowledge does not. In real contracts, the source definition fixes only about half of the errors. (3) Given an answer key, a small model can rank questions by how likely larger models are to get them wrong. Compositional diffusion planners extend short-horizon trajectory models to long-horizon goal-conditioned planning by generating and stitching multiple local trajectory chunks. However, in multimodal environments, local plausibility is not sufficient for global consistency: independently initialized chunks may denoise toward incompatible overlap modes, producing plans that are individually feasible but incoherent after stitching. We instead investigate graph-structured dependence among chunk-wise initial diffusion noises as a lightweight coordination mechanism for compositional trajectory planning. This preserves the standard-Gaussian initialization marginal of each chunk while biasing adjacent chunks toward compatible overlap-mode choices. We prove exact marginal preservation and show, under a simple multimodal overlap model, that positive coupling increases the probability of compatible mode selection. Mathematical proof assistants such as Coq, Agda, and Lean 4 build proof irrelevance into definitional conversion ($p \equiv q$ for $p, q : P$ with $P : Prop$), erasing which derivation established a theorem. While natural for mathematics over fixed axioms, judgmental proof irrelevance is incompatible with verified scientific stores, which must inspect and compare independent derivations of the same claim to assess consilience and compute counterfactual retractions when an assay, reagent, or model is invalidated. Moreover, combining definitional proof irrelevance with subsingleton elimination breaks strong normalization and renders algorithmic conversion non-transitive.
  We present the type-theoretic and institutional foundations of Eigenius, a verified knowledge substrate for science. First, we formulate a dependent kernel with cumulative universes ($Prop, Set, Type_k$), impredicative $Prop$, inductive families with index-forced subsingleton elimination, and intensional identity types, where definitional equality $\equiv$ is untyped joinability under an orthogonal CRS and proof irrelevance is a witnessed propositional axiom ($irrel : ΠP{:}Prop.\,Πp,q{:}P.\,Id\,P\,p\,q$). Second, we unify universe cumulativity, codomain-covariant $Π$-subtyping, and ontology class inclusions in a coercion-free subsumption judgment whose bidirectional fragments ($\mathcal{K}_0, \mathcal{K}_1$) satisfy subject reduction and are sound and complete for declarative typing. Third, we organize external verifiers into a three-level institutional hierarchy with explicit axiom policies (Adopt, Discharge, Relativize), internalize a justification logic with Why-provenance semiring support $supp(j,P)$, and embed Cooper's Type Theory with Records (TTR) to type open-world knowledge graphs. Finally, we prove consistency in Grothendieck universes and mechanize the metatheory in Lean 4 with zero sorry axioms.

##### **QSim-Arena: an evolving developer for quantum simulation advantage**
2611.15000v1 by Jue Xu, Yue Wang, Qi Zhao

Where practical advantage emerges in quantum dynamics simulation remains a central debate in quantum computing. Claimed advantages can be rapidly narrowed or overturned by stronger classical methods, while progress on both sides can outpace the journal publication cycle. We present QSim-Arena, a research framework in which AI agents combine a closed loop of verification and benchmarking with open exploration. Hamiltonians, initial states and accuracy targets for evolved states or observable expectation values provide explicit specifications for evaluating simulation methods. Agents translate published techniques into executable components, reproduce reported results and benchmark their performance across diverse Hamiltonian families. Competing classical methods are implemented and independently crosschecked, providing credible baselines for end-to-end quantum resource estimates based on rigorous error bounds and empirical calibration. Alongside this closed loop, agents recombine classical simulation techniques and co-design fault-tolerant quantum circuits using knowledge extracted from the literature. Initial applications improve baseline resource estimates through the integration of established techniques. QSim-Arena thus treats quantum advantage assessment as an evolving, reproducible research process that can respond to advances in algorithms, error analysis and hardware.

##### **MuEdit: An efficient multi-task editing method towards inter-domain knowledge conflicts**
2611.14910v1 by Songshi Liang, Ting-En Lin, Zixuan Huang, Yuchuan Wu, Yongbin Li, Zihe Wang, Rui Yan

Large language models (LLMs) require frequent knowledge updates across heterogeneous domains, but existing editing methods fail when applied sequentially to multi-task settings. We trace this collapse to inter-domain null-space misalignment: heterogeneous tasks induce distinct Jacobian geometries, causing their common null space to shrink dramatically and leaving insufficient admissible directions for conflict-free updates. We propose the Conflict Index to quantify this geometric interference, and introduce Mu-Edit, which mitigates multi-task conflicts via (1) conflict-aware ordering to minimize cumulative interference, and (2) dynamic low-rank approximation to expand the null space when ordering alone is insufficient. Experiments across five functional domains and three backbones show that Mu-Edit significantly outperforms existing baselines in multi-task editing performance while preserving general capabilities, and remains effective under incremental task arrival.

##### **Is Jev a Good Multilingual Judge?**
2611.14861v1 by Wei Liu, Yi Fan, Xiyan Fu

Generative large language models are widely used for multilingual text evaluation, yet whether decision models that directly output judgment probabilities can serve as multilingual judges has received limited attention. We evaluate Jev against generative LLM judges on four multilingual benchmarks with human annotations, covering up to 47 language varieties and three evaluation formats: binary decisions, pairwise comparisons, and graded ratings. At approximately $1/31$ to $1/61$ of GPT-6.1's evaluation cost, Jev is competitive on most multilingual judging tasks, but falls behind GPT-6.1 on knowledge-intensive answer verification. Further analysis shows that Jev is more likely to accept incorrect answers in low-resource languages than in English and that its probability outputs help identify errors across tasks. Based on these findings, we offer recommendations on evaluation instructions, confidence-based selection, and model routing, providing empirical guidance for using Jev as a multilingual judge.

Successful interaction requires understanding human perspectives, coordinating complementary capabilities, and maintaining productive relationships within socio-technical environments. This paper examines how foundations developed by the Autonomous Agents and Multi-Agent Systems (AAMAS) community inform these demands. We consider interactions ranging from individual human-agent partnerships to teams and larger groups, where participants differ in their roles, decision authority, capabilities, and objectives. We organize computational approaches around the connected challenges of understanding others and deciding how to interact with them. Together with shared autonomy, argumentation, and cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, these approaches offer foundations for agentic systems that account for evolving human knowledge, intentions, and expectations. We argue that progress requires treating human interaction as an ongoing decision problem shaped by uncertainty, interdependence, and potentially conflicting objectives.

Our harness, AutoAssumption, relaxes one assumption of a published method, re-derives and re-proves it, and generates its code. The eviction algorithm has the highest RULER-4K score of the published methods we ran, and the pruning algorithm the lowest perplexity of thirteen on four LLaMA models. ##### **Mach2: Scientific Discovery Platform with Deterministic Execution Constraints**
2611.14777v1 by Vansh Sharma, Venkat Raman

Domain requirements are represented as executable predicates: mandatory failures prevent advancement of the checked operation, while diagnostic findings are retained for correction or review. User-specified test-driven development (TDD), CodeGrapher's abstract-syntax-tree analysis, and history-guided evolutionary search provide complementary verification and optimization mechanisms. The originating harness validates returned artifacts against declared interfaces and evidence requirements without assuming continuous supervision of the remote run. This paper formalizes the rule-gated execution model and the agent-package lifecycle, and proposes measurable criteria for evaluating rule compliance, engineering validity, and cross-endpoint task continuity.

Full-duplex spoken dialogue, in which a system listens while it speaks and can backchannel, yield the floor, and be interrupted, is usually obtained by training on hundreds of thousands to millions of hours of speech. This is costly, rewrites the language model that supplies the system's knowledge, and still leaves turn-taking either implicit or decided coarsely rather than word by word. We show that a frozen, general-purpose multimodal LLM can instead be adapted into a word-level full-duplex system with little data. Our system is a thinker-talker cascade on an 80 ms frame clock. The thinker keeps the LLM frozen and trains only lightweight adapters and a few new token embeddings (97M parameters); at every frame it emits one token of agent text, with pauses, yields, and onsets represented as tokens of the same vocabulary and kept legal by a finite-state machine. A four-stage curriculum, each stage anchored to the model's own behavior under the previous format, performs the conversion in about 30 GPU-hours. The talker reads only the thinker's discrete tokens, so the two halves are trained independently. On Full-Duplex-Bench and InteractionBench, the system is competitive with open-source full-duplex systems built through large-scale speech training: among them, it is the least likely to take the floor when the user merely pauses, backchannels most often, and leads in spoken-task accuracy, groundedness, and coherence over long conversations.

##### **Mechanistic Interpretability Needs a Logic of Inference**
2611.14483v1 by Zhimin Hu, Chen Jiang, Hanbo Xie, N. Apurva Ratan Murty, Sashank Varma

Neural networks are increasingly studied not merely as predictive systems, but as complex systems whose internal computations are themselves objects of scientific inquiry. Recent progress in mechanistic interpretability (MI) has made it possible to identify neurons, features, circuits, population structures, and dynamical patterns associated with model behavior. As these tools improve, attention is shifting from what can be observed inside a model to how those observations support mechanistic explanations. We examine the parallel efforts of neuroscience and MI to address this question and identify recurring inferential bottlenecks when inferring mechanisms from complex neural systems. We propose five principles and organize them into a recursive logic for a more rigorous procedure of mechanistic inference. These principles provide a framework that supports scientific discovery in two senses: it helps turn local interpretability findings into cumulative knowledge of mechanisms within models, and it makes those mechanisms sufficiently precise to generate testable hypotheses about domains beyond the models. Evidence beyond MI is ultimately required to establish what model mechanisms reveal about the world.

##### **Semantic Chains: From Reachable Facts to Justified Answers**
2611.14252v1 by Misha Andreev

How can the facts that justify an answer be found from a question and presented as a sequence of connected statements? This paper models a context as a graph of logical facts and takes as given which expressions represent each fact. A parent fact can express a relation among its subfacts, and a preserving edge allows the content of a subfact to be recovered within a full representation of its parent. Suppose a root fact justifies the full answer and a path of preserving edges leads from that root to a fact that designates an object shared by the question and the answer. Under the conditions of the model, a search starting from the objects in the question reaches the root and covers every fact in the selected expansion. The root then admits a text that forms a single link connecting the question to the full answer and justifying it. For chains with several links, the paper gives a complete enumeration of candidate orders within the stated class and specifies when the link texts jointly preserve the justification and support every transition.

#### Abstracts
##### **WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers**
2611.18553v1 by Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi

Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available at https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

摘要：

##### **Elliptic Sampling: Optimal Interpolant Schedules for Gaussians and Beyond**
2611.18542v1 by Cole Becker, Taylan Kargin, Kimia Hassibi, Asuman Ozdaglar, Pablo A. Parrilo

Generative modeling based on stochastic interpolants requires specifying two key choices: an interpolant schedule bridging the initial and target distributions, and an efficient discretization of the resulting probability-flow ODE. In this work, we introduce a new framework for joint schedule/discretization design based on the analyticity properties of the (complexified) ODE trajectories, which in turn ensure good numerical behavior. Our formulation can be solved exactly via conformal mapping, yielding a natural family of variance-preserving elliptic schedules, parametrized by Jacobi elliptic functions. Pairing an elliptic schedule with a tailored discretization, we obtain Zoloflow, a new sampling algorithm closely connected to Zolotarev's optimal rational function approximants. Crucially, both the elliptic schedule and Zoloflow explicitly incorporate the condition number $κ$ of the target distribution and reduce to the classical trig schedule in the limit $κ\rightarrow 1$. For Gaussian targets, Zoloflow achieves minimax optimality among all oblivious score-span algorithms, attaining total-variation error $\varepsilon$ using $Θ(\logκ\,\log(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$ (exact) score queries. For the log-concave case, the elliptic schedule allows sampling with at most $\varepsilon$ 2-Wasserstein-error by requiring merely $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d} \, \mathrm{polylog}(κ, 1/\varepsilon))$ score evaluations. Complementing these theoretical results, we empirically evaluate the elliptic schedule on general (non-log-concave) target distributions, including detailed comparisons with alternative schedule proposals on both synthetic benchmarks and existing image generation datasets.

摘要：

##### **ModHarness-RL: Recasting Harnesses as Learnable and Evolving Programs for Agentic RL**
2611.18533v1 by Zelin He, Siyu Zhu, Jacob Helwig, Yujian Liu, Li An, Rohit Jain, Shiyu Chang, Alborz Geramifard

Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly relies on a harness, the scaffold around the model that decides what it sees of its own interaction and what guidance it receives. Most existing approaches train under a hand-written harness that stays fixed throughout. However, an effective harness depends on the domain, the data and the model, and keeps changing as the model trains. We introduce ModHarness-RL, which recasts the harness as programs that are learned from data and evolve as the model trains with RL. ModHarness-RL records the interaction as structured State, a set of typed blocks, and two core components of the harness are programs over these blocks, a Compression program that assembles the accumulated history into the model's context and a Steering program that supplies guidance when behavior deviates. During RL training, candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories, so the harness tracks how the model's behavior shifts. We study CodeSearch in depth and further analyze ALFWorld and BIRD SQL, with models from 1.5B to 8B. The proposed Compression and Steering mechanisms improve held-out performance over the fixed-harness baseline in most settings. These results suggest that a harness for agentic RL can be learned from the task's data and evolved with the model.

摘要：

##### **Alignment by Socialization: Learning Tradeoffs Under Selection**
2611.18531v1 by Thomas Brennan, Andrew W. Lo, Chaoyi Zhao

Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are expected to complete tasks while following rules of conduct, but these objectives can conflict. We study how evolutionary selection shapes an agent's choices between them, and whether experience can make those choices reflect human judgments and withstand subsequent pressures. In our model, copies of an agent face a common uncertain reward state, and only those taking the rewarded action reproduce. We show that long-run growth is maximized when each action is taken as often as the environment rewards it. We compare three policies. A task-only policy thrives in a sandbox rewarding completion alone but fails when conduct matters. A fixed linear policy increasingly favors task completion as its stakes rise, even where humans never reward it. A learned policy instead converges to the tradeoffs rewarded by its environment; when humans supply the rewards, we call this socialization. Accumulated experience makes socialization harder to reverse under subsequent task-only training, although violations can arise before the prevailing disposition changes, and finite memory weakens this protection. When a single violation can end a deployment, a hard prohibition can prevent it, but a mistaken prohibition can also be costly. Experiments with small neural networks illustrate how training targets and memory shape these outcomes. Our results suggest that alignment depends not only on the objectives assigned to agents, but also on the environments that shape and sustain their behavior.

##### **Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors**
2611.18485v1 by Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell

This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($π_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

##### **Dancing Tokens on the Vocabulary Network from LLM Generation**
2611.18248v1 by Yuan-Chao Hu

Large language models (LLMs) generate text one token at a time, yet how a particular generation relates to the geometry learned in pre-training remains unclear. The fundamental explainability of LLMs is elusive. Here, we build a similarity network from the input embeddings of six open LLMs and map generation onto it. The token-embedding network is scale-free while heavy-tailed, as are random embeddings with matched covariance, but far more fragmented. Distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited. The knowledge space is thus deterministic from pre-training. Consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbours and, in most models, no more often than in shuffled order, so generation is not a walk on the predefined network, although the tokens it visits are concentrated within it. Through the logit lens in a layer- wise manner, tokens settle late in depth in most models, and repetitive models remain confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs. These observables could serve as inference-time monitors of degeneration. Closed-loop simulations show that, under matched energy-throughput requirements, the agent achieves longer cycling life than conventional charging baselines and optimization-based strategies across heterogeneous battery configurations. This study provides a simulation-grounded proof of concept for using LLM agents as sequential decision makers in battery management and takes a step toward language-model-enabled control of physical energy systems.

In this work we present a quantum-hybrid optimization approach for planning drilling trajectories on multi-material structures, with the objective of minimizing total energy consumption. TRIBE v2 (d'Ascoli et al., 2026) predicts fMRI responses across the cortical surface from video, audio and text. In contrast, motion selectivity is strong and precisely localized: 22% of the 1% most motion-selective vertices fall in MT+, which covers 0.42% of cortex. 2611.18104v1 by Zailong Tian

The fingerprint by which a classifier recognizes the LLM behind a text is usually treated as a fixed property of the model. After such a handoff, detection of the substitute by a text classifier drops from 0.97 to 0.72 (DeepSeek replacing Claude Sonnet) and from 0.87 to 0.54 for a same-vendor downgrade (Haiku), and retraining on handoff data repairs only substitutes seen in training. With contemporaneous references on two closed models, its false-positive rate is 0.056 (preregistered bound 0.10), and it detects 0.81 of substitute sessions across six pairs after four audited turns.

Differential association theory and its extension, social learning theory, hold that rule-breaking, and the definitions that make it acceptable, are learned through interaction with others. In a preregistered experiment, 420 teams of three agents (1,260 agents; seven AI models) worked on coding tasks that could not be solved honestly. Compared with a silent peer, a deviant peer increased cheating (66.2% vs. 62.9%; OR = 1.96) and an honest peer reduced it (55.7%; OR = 0.35). Among agents that cheated, a deviant peer increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%), often as the team's norm. Monitoring did not suppress prohibited discussion; it doubled it. ##### **Deep Latents, Long Memory: Latent persistence explains temporal correlations in transformer activations**
2611.18089v1 by Francesco Cagnetta, Santiago Acevedo

The hidden layers of Large Language Models (LLMs) are believed to encode representations of the data whose abstraction increases with depth. Here, we propose an alternative view, building on the principle that the correlations of a sequence reflect the structure of the process that generated it. We define the autocorrelation function of a layer as the average dot product between its activations at two context positions separated by a lag. Latent variables encoded persistently along the context create slowly decaying tails in the autocorrelation, and we find these tails to grow across layers in both synthetic and natural data. In synthetic data with controlled hierarchical latent structure, this growth stems from the encoding of abstract latent variables, which govern long stretches of the input. In real text, we take sparse-autoencoder features as proxies for latent variables, and find that their persistence correlates strongly with the magnitude of autocorrelations across datasets and LLMs. In particular, the persistence time increases with depth, as do autocorrelations, implying that deeper latent features persist for longer context spans. 2611.18030v1 by Rui Rua, Hailemariam Mersha, Joao Paulo Fernandes, Karim Ali

Industry now relies on LLMs to write a large share of production code. ##### **Toward Universal Translatability: On the Statistical Ontology of Generative AI**
2611.17963v1 by Katia Schwerzmann

This paper argues that the problem of translation functions both as a point of origin and as a normative horizon for contemporary generative AI. In current discourse around ''world models,'' their attainment is framed as the achievement of internal consistency, cross-modal alignment, and physical grounding. Taken together, these demands converge on an ideal of the model as a universal translation machine capable of establishing lossless correspondences across linguistic, visual, and physical representation spaces. I show that this ideal presupposes a metaphysical commitment that I call a statistical ontology: the belief that reality has an underlying, computable statistical structure that can be recovered through sufficient data and modeling, rather than being shaped and accessed through situated human interpretation and labor. Tracing this commitment back to Warren Weaver's 1949 Memorandum on machine translation, I outline how translation was historically recast as code deciphering and, later, as the alignment of probability distributions across structured outputs. Finally, I contrast this paradigm with approaches in Translation Studies that foreground opacity, power asymmetries, and the translator's positionality. I argue that generative AI's statistical ontology is pre-Kantian, pre-critical and obscures the infrastructures and human labor that make machine translatability---as alignment---possible in the first place.

Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.

##### **Measured from the feed: the unsustainable growth of AI video generation**
2611.17938v1 by Tristan Coignion, Aurélie Bugeau, Gael Guennebaud, Anne-Laure Ligozat

Text-to-video generation runs at commercial scale, but its broader environmental impacts remain, to our knowledge, understudied. Providers rarely disclose video volumes or energy needs; we measured the yearly video output of two commercial platforms and built a prospective model of such services' absolute footprint from training to storage. Our projections suggest that reducing emissions depends on both limiting growth in video generation and achieving substantial efficiency gains. Under continued growth, emissions rise nearly seventy-five-fold by 2030 despite technological improvements. When usage remains at 2026 levels, the same improvements reduce emissions by about two-fifths. These estimates are a conservative lower bound: they cover one AI service among many; other services and uses would push the total higher.

##### **Tracing Actual Causes with Counterfactual Witness Maps**
2611.17937v1 by Tim Woydt, Matej Zečević, Kristian Kersting, Moritz Willig

Halpernian actual causation, the formal study of which events in a specific situation caused a specific outcome in acyclic structural causal models, underpins legal responsibility, moral blame, and the assessment of causal harm. Despite the rich logical framework, no prior sound and complete enumeration procedure is known for finding all actual causes in a given setting, blocking downstream tasks such as responsibility attribution, blame assessment, and causal-harm grading that aggregate over the full cause set. We close this gap by introducing witness targets: sets of joint configurations containing every counterfactual witness. In combination with an ancestor intervention grammar we derive a finite directed acyclic witness map. We prove that for every actual cause there exists a contingency set such that the joint intervention set appears as a node of this map, and present an algorithm for Tracing Actual Causes (TrAC) that is sound and complete for enumerating all actual causes of any target event. We compare TrAC empirically with existing single-cause baselines on random Boolean structural causal models, demonstrating practical feasibility for graphs of up to 15 nodes.

##### **Condensing Law of Security Information**
2611.17918v1 by Qi Tan, Tianyu Cui, Qi Li, Ke Xu

Scaling laws indicate that the generalization error of a large language model (LLM) decreases monotonically with model size once data is sufficient, and the corollary that larger models outperform smaller ones is now widely accepted. We argue that this corollary fails in security: decisions there hinge on anomalous inputs, which are long-tailed (e.g., zero-day attacks) and therefore poorly served by likelihood fitting. Worse still, the capability brought about by scale (e.g., inferring the intended whole from a corrupted part) leads a LLM silently \emph{repair} the anomaly before any downstream security head ever sees it. We formalize this as a tradeoff between two quantities: the cognitive capability $K(W)=I(W;P)$ that a model holds about the world distribution, and the security-information retention $R(W)=\frac{I(Z;S)}{I(A;S)}$ that survives in its intermediate representation. We derive three information-theoretic bounds to connect them: an upper bound on restoration accuracy in terms of $K(W)$, an upper bound on $R(W)$ in terms of the restoration error, and a Fano lower bound on security-task error in terms of $R(W)$. These theorems approximately imply that usable security information is a product of how much security information survives and how well it can be extracted, indicating that optimal usable security information can be achieved at a certain model size. We state this as the \emph{condensing law of security information}: usable security information is unimodal in model size, so security generalization error is U-shaped and is minimized at a finite $N^{\star}$ rather than decreasing without bound. The experiments on various real-world LLMs validate the condensing law, indicating that there is an optimal model size to solve security issues.

As Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based systems have become increasingly integrated into various aspects of everyday life, including education, understanding how these systems make decisions is of paramount importance. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. 2611.17854v1 by Bo Qin, Zeyun Yang, Qiaofei Xue, Yanan Ding, Jinyu Wang, Chaopeng Ji, Shijin Yuan, Bin Mu, Wansuo Duan, Guokun Dai, Mu Mu, Yi Deng

The predictability of the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) has historically been linked to large-scale errors in models' initial conditions such as heat content and surface wind stress. Here we demonstrate that these slowly evolving errors may ultimately originate from mesoscale errors in initial oceanic states that are common in current models and ocean observation systems. This hidden source of ENSO forecast uncertainty, in terms of mesoscale ocean-current errors, is convincingly exposed by the conditional nonlinear optimal perturbation (CNOP) method applied to a differentiable ocean AI model. The CNOP-identified most influential current errors are strongly event-dependent. For the 1997/98 El Niño, the initial current errors in the eastern Pacific results in the largest forecast error through locally suppressing original subsurface warming. For the 2015/16 event, the dominate error originates from the western Pacific, which reorganizes upper ocean temperature structure and affects the eastern Pacific through the equatorial waveguide. Further analysis reveals that these current errors perturb mesoscale eddy count and eddy kinetic energy triggering large-scale temperature errors that hinder El Niño development. The timing of the maximum growth rate of the obtained CNOP error is well aligned with the spring predictability barrier of ENSO, and the error structures are reflected in simulated analysis errors caused by unresolved mesoscale currents. These findings extend the potential source of ENSO predictability beyond large-scale thermal signals, reveal for the first time an uncharted route of ENSO forecast failure, and provide an important physical basis for targeted ocean observations and data assimilation in the future.

摘要：

##### **Is Abliteration Getting Harder? Not Uniformly: Refusal Removal by Direction-Based Ablation and ARA in Heretic on the Qwen3.8, Gemma 4, Olmo 3.1 and Mistral Small 3.2 LLMs Versus Their Predecessors**
2611.17846v1 by Richard J. Young

Abliteration, a fine-tuning-free weight edit, removes refusal from open-weight large language models (LLMs). The developer of Arbitrary-Rank Ablation (ARA), an optimisation-based variant, reports that direction-based abliteration, which ablates a refusal direction, increasingly fails on newer models. On five release pairs, refusal removal did not become uniformly harder. Qwen2.5-32B and Qwen3.8-27B, Gemma-2-27B and Gemma-4-31B, OLMo-2-32B and Olmo-3.1-32B, Mistral-Small-2409 and Mistral-Small-3.2, and Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3.5-4B were edited in Heretic 2.0 by three or four direction-based methods (difference-in-means, MPOA, SOM and, on the small pair, J-space) and ARA under the same prompts, nominal search budget, first-token KL divergence cap and selection rule; removal counted as harder when the automatic WildGuard judge found more of 972 held-out harmful prompts (JailbreakBench, XSTest, StrongREJECT, HarmBench and an author-compiled malicious-code bank) still refused. After direction-based removal, newer releases retained -1 to +37 percentage points more refusal, and after ARA -26 to +34. With run-to-run variance included, between-pair heterogeneity of the direction-based effect ranged from high to low with the scale and variance model (I^2 20-80%). ARA lost less effectiveness than direction-based methods in Gemma and OLMo and more in Qwen; each verdict rests on one ARA run, and some changed with the divergence budget or variance model. In Gemma the direction-based increase came from the malicious-code bank and reversed on public sets. Contrary to prediction, keyword counts at the tool's window did not undercount judged refusal on average, but differed by a mean 7 points per model. Audits of a single pair, method or prompt source can reach opposite conclusions. Whether a newer model resisted removal depended on the release pair, method and budget, not on generation alone.

摘要：

##### **BLiMP-RO: A Benchmark of Linguistic Minimal Pairs for Romanian**
2611.17836v1 by Radu-Stefan Popa, Valentin Feroiu, Adina Camelia Bleotu, Sergiu Nisioi

Evaluating grammatical knowledge in language models requires benchmarks that cover diverse linguistic phenomena and are validated against human judgments. We introduce BLiMP-RO, a Romanian minimal-pair benchmark covering 50 grammatical phenomena across ten domains. It comprises 493 linguist-constructed sentence pairs, evaluated by native speakers in five experiments, and 5,942 additional semi-automatically generated, human-validated pairs. We further investigate grammatical generalization under limited linguistic input by training GPT-2 and GPT-BERT models from scratch on Romanian BabyBabelLM data and a newly collected corpus of 10 million words from child-targeted video captions. Increasing caption training data yields approximately 9 percentage points of accuracy improvement per tenfold increase in corpus size. At matched data sizes, BabyBabelLM generally outperforms video captions, while GPT-BERT consistently outperforms GPT-2. Our findings highlight how training data quantity, composition, and model architecture shape grammatical generalization in Romanian.

摘要：

##### **PaperCritic: Training AI Reviewers to Assess the Soundness of Papers**
2611.17834v1 by Hyun Ryu, Ji Yong Cho, Eunho Yang, Moontae Lee, June Yong Yang, Sean Welleck, Carolyn Rose

As AI conference submissions grow rapidly, AI reviewers are being considered as an alternative. However, simply imitating human reviewing is problematic because reviewing serves multiple purposes, including evaluating papers' soundness, interestingness, and clarity, the last two of which are subjective. In this work, we focus solely on evaluating soundness, the most important and objective criterion, and define the learning objective as jointly maximizing (1) precision, based on the soundness and substantiveness of review points, and (2) recall, the coverage of high-quality reference review points. Under this problem formulation, we construct a quality-labeled review point dataset, PaperSound-23K, using 1K ICLR submissions and 23K human-written and LLM-generated review points. Using this dataset, we train an AI reviewer model, PaperCritic, through reinforcement learning with the F1 score of precision and recall as the reward. Our experiments show that PaperCritic outperforms frontier models and models trained with other AI reviewer training methods on an in-domain test set. Surprisingly, learning to review ICLR submissions also transfers to reviewing Nature-family papers and even to out-of-domain reviewing tasks (e.g., code review, legal contract review, and feedback on story writing).

摘要：

##### **V-JEPA-LM: Video-Language Models That Read Dynamics Beyond Static, Text-Aligned Representations**
2611.17831v1 by Keuntae Kim, Dongjun Kim, Yong Suk Choi

World-model video encoders such as V-JEPA learn how scenes change (direction, speed, order, state) from video alone. To answer questions, they are joined to a language model trained on captions and question-answer pairs. But each caption or question names only a few attributes of a clip, so the model learns to retrieve those attributes rather than to read the clip, and the dynamics the encoder carries go unused: on the same items, a linear probe reads speed from frozen V-JEPA features at 87% while the VLM answers at chance. We show where dynamics lives in a frozen encoder: in a subspace shared across videos for predictive encoders such as V-JEPA, but not as a shared component of frame-wise, text-aligned encoders such as SigLIP. We then build counterfactual supervision that makes the language model read it: pixel transformations turn public videos into answer-balanced variants whose answers are set by the transformation and differ only in the change. Trained in a single stage on 4.53M public samples with 32 visual tokens per frame, where open video LLMs use several stages, 9M to 172M samples and 64 to 256 tokens per frame on average, the resulting V-JEPA-LM models (1B to 8B, Qwen3 and Llama) lead every size class in average score across eleven video benchmarks; our 4B model scores 72.1, against 61.1 for the best open 4B and 64.8 for the best open 8B model. A controlled encoder x supervision study shows that the encoder decides what can be read and the supervision what is read: at a matched budget, counterfactual supervision adds 4.5 points on average and 38 on the speed questions of natural benchmarks. Code and models: https://github.com/kkt94/Llama-V-JEPA-LM.

摘要：

##### **Aligning AI Teams**
2611.17820v1 by Siddarth Srinivasan, Morgan Jane Matthews, Jascha Sohl-Dickstein, Erik Jones

Previous work has shown that alignment training on individual LLM agents can fail to transfer to multi-agent settings. We investigate the underlying mechanisms for this failure, and explore interventions to preserve alignment transfer to multi-agent LLM teams. We focus on two software-engineering tasks (sepsis triage, a news recommender) and ten consulting-proposal generation tasks. We present four findings: (1) The single-vs-team safety gap does not show a clear trend with more capable models. We find that Anthropic's Mythos Preview shows the largest gap on one of our tasks but the smallest on another; (2) Many natural interventions, such as having agents critique each other's work or coordinate in a group chat, do not reliably bring agent teams back to single-agent alignment behavior; (3) diffusion of responsibility substantially accounts for the safety gap: agents in teams are less likely to proactively check for system-level safety failures and more likely to ignore, rationalize, or deflect responsibility for issues when they arise; (4) having a designated safety lead agent ensure system-level safety, and having a coordinator agent publish a safety specifications document are effective at restoring single-agent alignment. We also release magelab, a multi-agent LLM orchestration and experimentation framework. Our findings can help practitioners design agent teams that successfully preserve the behaviors expected of aligned individual agents.

摘要：

##### **Causally Constrained and Uncertainty-Aware Cardiopulmonary Risk Prediction**
2611.17819v1 by Yuxiang Lai, Jiashuo Zhang, Liang Zhao, Xiaofeng Yang

Routine chest computed tomography (CT) captures pulmonary, cardiac, and vascular phenotypes that jointly stratify lung cancer and cardiovascular disease, the two leading causes of death in lung-screening populations. Yet clinical risk calculators evaluate each disease in isolation and omit CT findings, whereas unconstrained machine learning overfits small hospital cohorts, learns treatment- and illness-confounded risk directions, and conceals uncertainty when patient records are incomplete. Here we present the Causal Envelope Agent (CE-Agent), a two-stage framework that compiles peer-reviewed epidemiological evidence into explicit constraints on risk-factor weights before any outcome is seen, while estimating local baseline risk and newly added CT phenotypes from cohort data. At deployment, closed-form uncertainty propagation accounts for missing clinical or CT inputs within milliseconds per patient, running over two orders of magnitude faster than Monte Carlo sampling and returning calibrated risk intervals that direct chart review and selective imaging to borderline patients. Across 53,452 lung-screening participants from 33 US hospitals and 6,814 multi-ethnic adults, CE-Agent outperforms cardiovascular calculators in lung screening, exceeds the Pooled Cohort Equations on hard atherosclerotic events while correcting their 1.7-fold risk overestimation, and generalizes from a single hospital or CT scanner to external sites while lowering required sample sizes up to fivefold. Applied unchanged to lung cancer, CE-Agent matches established screening models with accurate calibration even when restricted to risk-factor directions alone, and pairing both predictions improves joint cardiopulmonary triage from a single screening visit.

摘要：

##### **G-CAST: Governed Continual Self-Training for E-Commerce Agents under Evolving Demands**
2611.17814v1 by Yuechun Yu, Wenzheng Kong, Xiaofeng Mao, Jin Zhang

With the rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs), an increasing number of users are turning to LLM-powered agents for conversational shopping in e-commerce. However, the user needs continue to change as new product categories, procurement scenarios, and feature requests emerge. At the same time, the product's execution harness-including its tools, skills, and operating procedures-continues to evolve to support increasingly sophisticated interactions. These changes create two practical challenges: 1) Demand drift alters the distribution, intent, and complexity of user requests, causing a model trained once to fall behind; 2) Harness evolution requires the model to use newly introduced capabilities correctly without losing behavior that users already rely on. Periodic manual retraining is too delayed and labor-intensive for this setting. To address these challenges, we present G-CAST, a governed continual self-training system that converts daily production evidence under evolving demand and capability contracts into reward-routed, entropy-prioritized training data and governed reward evolution. Governed checkpoint transition under cumulative evaluation controls each model update, enabling the agent to learn newly introduced behaviors without sacrificing previously accepted capabilities. Experiments on a real production e-commerce agent show that G-CAST achieves stronger adaptation to newly introduced demand while better preserving previously accepted behavior compared with a strong autonomous post-training baseline. Ablations further show that both reward-routed, entropy-prioritized data orchestration and governed reward evolution are important to these gains. G-CAST is now deployed in Yuancai(http://cai.1688.com/), a B2B procurement agent on Alibaba's 1688 platform.

#### Abstracts
##### **Can Mechanistic Interpretability Help Explain LLM Agent Failures? A Case Study on Web Element Hallucination**
2611.18514v1 by Mohamed Aghzal, Mor Geva, Gregory J. Stein, Ziyu Yao

Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has made significant progress in explaining the internal computations of Large Language Models (LLMs); however, these advances have yet to demonstrate comparable success in explaining the decision-making processes of LLM-based agents. This paper examines the challenges MI tools face when applied to realistic agentic failures. We focus on one such failure: web agents hallucinating actions that are not available on a website. We apply a series of mechanistic analyzes to Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as it interacts with live websites. We find that a linear probe trained on the last prompt token can predict hallucinations before generation begins (0.80 AUC) and steering along a probe direction corrects 85\% of hallucinations, but disrupts 7\% of valid actions. However, these results only provide a partial explanation of why the agent hallucinates. The probe direction is sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to how actions are represented. Additionally, circuit identification can be difficult as the computation is distributed across multiple components and positions, and the information the agent relies on varies across examples. We discuss how these observations challenge the assumptions underlying standard MI methods for circuit discovery and feature identification, and outline future directions for making these methods better suited to explain failures in LLM-based agents.

摘要：

##### **From AI-Enabled Remote Patient Monitoring Toward Agentic Robotics: Embodiment, Governance, and Care Integration**
2611.18484v1 by Ivana Ragusa, Lorenzo Guiducci, Filippo Cavallo

AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. We propose agentic robotics as a future direction for supervised systems embedded in the care ecosystem, connecting AI agents and robots with clinicians, caregivers, and patients through auditable workflows that can be interrupted and remain subject to human oversight and explicit AI governance.

摘要：

##### **Towards Explainable Sleep Stage Classification using Single-Channel Electroencephalogram**
2611.18434v1 by Gurinder Kaur Sandhu, Jude Koenig, Ankita Shukla, Shruti Kshirsagar

Automated sleep staging has achieved strong bench- mark accuracy; however, clinical adoption remains limited due to a lack of interpretability. In this work, we fill the research gap by extending the SleepXAI sleep staging framework to systematically compare five different XAI methods. Further, attribution fidelity is evaluated using Spearman rank correlation with AASM-defined EEG frequency bands, serving as clinically grounded reference signals. In this paper, we focus on single- channel Electroencephalogram (EEG), which is more amenable to practical clinical deployment compared to full polysomnography setups. Experiments are conducted on the Sleep-EDF benchmark dataset and the DREAMT dataset. In DREAMT, we stratified subjects based on OSA into four Apnea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) severity groups. We observed that the SleepXAI model achieves strong classification performance on both datasets. Among the evaluated methods, Integrated Gradients (IG) consistently achieves the highest mean attribution fidelity across all five sleep stages on Sleep-EDF and across all AHI groups on DREAMT, outperforming all other evaluated methods under the Spearman rank correlation metric. These findings highlight the importance of attribution method selection as a key factor for ensuring reliable and clinically interpretable automated sleep staging systems.

##### **AI-Based Multi-View Echocardiographic Image Synthesis using Dynamic Shape Control**
2611.18375v1 by João Freitas, Anca Balinisteanu, Ahmed Youssef, Vitor Hugo Pereira, Jan D'hooge, Sandro Queirós

Although automated methods have improved echocardiographic workflows, progress remains constrained by the need for large, well-annotated datasets. As the development of clinical databases is often limited by ethical and logistical challenges, synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising alternative. However, existing approaches commonly rely on left ventricle shape-based supervision or are tailored to specific cardiac views, limiting the generation of additional standard and unconventional imaging planes and the preservation of multi-view consistency across cardiac chambers. To address these limitations, we propose a novel framework for shape-controlled synthesis of multi-view echocardiographic sequences. First, multi-view cross-sections are extracted from dynamic whole-heart meshes. Ultrasound-like appearance is then introduced through a two-stage AI-based pipeline. In Stage 1, an unpaired GAN-diffusion model translates these cross-sections into realistic echocardiographic frames. As this translation is performed independently at the frame level, Stage 2 employs masked video reconstruction to improve temporal coherence and cardiac motion. Evaluation demonstrated high frame-level realism for both stages, while the integration of Stage 2 substantially improved video-level motion quality and temporal consistency. Overall, the proposed framework enables the generation of realistic multi-view echocardiographic sequences from diverse cardiac models, providing a scalable source of annotated data for dataset augmentation and the development of multi-view cardiac assessment methods.
